7:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Problem Domain & Taxonomy"}]
8:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Context"}]
9:["$","p",null,{"children":["Before 2023, vision-language alignment was bottlenecked by data cost.\nThe dominant recipe required either expensive annotation pipelines or narrow domain-specific datasets.\nLLaVA's contribution is to replace that bottleneck entirely: given a language-only GPT-4 and text descriptions of an image (",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-coco","children":"COCO"}]," captions and bounding boxes), the model bootstraps multimodal instruction-following data ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"without any human labeling of the instruction set"}],".\nThe resulting ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"LLaVA-Instruct-158K"}]," corpus became the template the modern VLM field inherits."]}]
a:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The pipeline."}],"\nGPT-4 never sees the image.\nEach image's COCO caption(s) and bounding-box annotations are serialized into text and passed to GPT-4 with a small set of seed in-context examples; GPT-4 then generates instruction-following data ",["$","em",null,{"children":"as if"}]," it had viewed the image.\nThree response types are collected, totaling ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"158K samples"}],":"]}]
b:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Conversation (~58K)"}]," — multi-turn Q&A about visible content."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Detailed description (~23K)"}]," — paragraph-length descriptions."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Complex reasoning (~77K)"}]," — multi-step reasoning chaining visual facts."]}],"\n"]}]

e:["$","p",null,{"children":"LLaVA sits in one of three families of vision-language fusion that coalesced around 2023.\nThe families differ in how visual features enter the language model:"}]
f:["$","table",null,{"children":[["$","thead",null,{"children":["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","th",null,{"children":"Family"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"Representative"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"Fusion mechanism"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"Trade-off"}]]}]}],["$","tbody",null,{"children":[["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Cross-attention"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Flamingo (DeepMind)"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Dedicated gated cross-attention layers inserted between LM blocks; LM attends to visual features as a separate key/value stream"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Preserves LM capacity; bolted-on — non-uniform with text attention"}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Query-based bottleneck"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"BLIP-2 (Q-Former)"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"A small set of learnable query tokens extracts image features via cross-attention, then concatenated as a prefix"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Compact (32 tokens); loses spatial detail"}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Patch-as-token concat"}],["$","td",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"LLaVA"}],", LLaVA-1.5"]}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Visual patches projected into LM token space, concatenated directly with text tokens, processed by the LLM's own self-attention"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Simple, reuses LM infrastructure; no spatial bottleneck; expensive in tokens (576 per image)"}]]}]]}]]}]

3e:["$","h3",null,{"children":"The Scaling Frontier & Empirical Trends"}]
3f:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Scaling Laws"}]
40:["$","p",null,{"children":"LLaVA's primary scaling move is input resolution: LLaVA-1.0 used CLIP ViT-L/14-224px, LLaVA-1.5 upgraded to CLIP ViT-L/14-336px.\nThe paper does not report a resolution-vs-performance curve, but downstream work — Prismatic RQ6 (Part II) — does ablate the axis and finds 384px is the sweet spot with diminishing returns beyond.\nPatch granularity (14×14) is fixed by the pretrained CLIP weights and is not explored in LLaVA."}]
41:["$","p",null,{"children":"LLM scaling is evaluated only coarsely: Vicuna-7B vs Vicuna-13B, with the 13B variant consistently winning but without a reported exponent or fit."}]
42:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Diminishing Returns"}]
43:["$","p",null,{"children":"At fixed 336px resolution and 14×14 patch size, the token budget is pinned at 576.\nThis is the information ceiling for the visual stream: more capacity in the LLM (13B vs 7B), more instruction-tuning data, or a better connector cannot push through it.\nLLaVA-1.6 (\"Next\") explored resolution tiling as a next step; downstream VLM families (GPT-4V, Claude 3 vision, Qwen-VL) have retained higher-resolution encoders as their primary visual-scaling axis."}]
44:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Robotic Grounding & Physicality Gap"}]
45:["$","h4",null,{"children":"The Precision Gap"}]
46:["$","p",null,{"children":"LLaVA is a batch-offline VLM.\nThe paper reports no FLOPs/token, no latency figures, no tokens-per-second on common accelerators.\nFine in principle — but the silence is significant: LLaVA's recipe (frozen CLIP ViT-L + 576-token projection + 7–13B LLM decode) is sized for chat-style VQA, not 10–200 Hz closed-loop control.\nAnything downstream that needs real-time control has to either (a) distill LLaVA into a smaller decoder, (b) cache visual features, or (c) replace the backbone with something sized for the task."}]
47:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Benchmark Critique"}]
48:["$","p",null,{"children":["LLaVA is evaluated entirely on static benchmarks (POPE, CHAIR, VQAv2, GQA, ScienceQA, TextVQA, MM-Vet).\nNone of these probe contact dynamics, multi-step object manipulation, temporal grounding, or failure recovery under perturbation.\nThe practical consequence is already visible in the downstream VLA literature — ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-openvla","children":"OpenVLA"}]," chose Prismatic (not LLaVA) as its backbone because Prismatic's fused SigLIP + DINOv2 features beat LLaVA by ~10% absolute success rate on BridgeData V2 language-grounding tasks (Part II, §3.3).\nThe LLaVA recipe is a starting point for robotics-grade VLMs, not an endpoint."]}]

4c:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Critical Synthesis & The \"Load-Bearing\" Audit"}]
4d:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Load-Bearing Assumptions"}]
4e:["$","p",null,{"children":"LLaVA's story rests on three assumptions that deserve scrutiny:"}]
4f:["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{}],"\n"]}]
50:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":["A language-only GPT-4 can generate ",["$","em",null,{"children":"useful"}]," multimodal instruction data from text descriptions alone."]}],"\nThe COCO captions + bounding-box serialization gives GPT-4 object identities, rough spatial layout, and counts — but not texture, material, lighting, or camera geometry.\nThe generated instructions inherit the representational blindness of the input.\nThis matters when downstream users expect the model to answer questions GPT-4 was never prompted to hallucinate.\n2.\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"A linear projection of CLIP features carries enough information for language-scale reasoning."}],"\nBounded above by rank ≤ 576 (per the theorem in §2).\nThis is true in dimensionality — but \"enough\" is task-dependent; it's the reason Prismatic's fused SigLIP + DINOv2 encoder wins on localization and OpenVLA's robotic policies prefer Prismatic to LLaVA.\n3.\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"An autoregressive language-modeling loss is sufficient to align visual and linguistic representations."}],"\nTrue for chat-style VQA; less so for grounded tasks.\nThe CLIP contrastive loss already discarded fine-grained geometry; no downstream LM loss recovers it."]}]
51:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Foundational vs. Marginal Gains"}]
52:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Foundational."}]," Visual Instruction Tuning is the foundational move.\nThe GPT-4 data-bootstrap pattern — use a strong language model to generate supervised instruction data that a smaller multimodal model can then train on — is now the dominant pipeline for modern VLMs (InstructBLIP, Prismatic's SFT mixture, Qwen-VL's instruction stage).\nWithout VIT, modern VLM training would still be bottlenecked by human-annotator cost."]}]
53:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Marginal."}]," The 1.0 → 1.5 architectural deltas (linear projection → 2-layer MLP, 224px → 336px) are engineering refinements, not conceptual advances.\nEach gives a few points on downstream benchmarks; neither changes what the model can fundamentally do.\nPrismatic's subsequent design-space audit (Part II) shows that within this architectural family the biggest remaining wins come from ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"encoder choice and fusion"}],", not connector width."]}]
54:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Failure Modes"}]
55:["$","p",null,{"children":"The quantitative failure-mode analysis LLaVA reports is hallucination.\nTwo benchmarks dominate the VLM hallucination literature: POPE (probing) and CHAIR (captioning)."}]
56:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"POPE (Polling-based Object Probing Evaluation; Li et al., EMNLP 2023)."}],"\nProbes object hallucination by asking the model yes/no questions of the form ",["$","em",null,{"children":"\"Is there a <object> in the image?\""}],"\nA balanced set of present and absent objects is sampled under three splits that stress different failure modes:"]}]
57:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Random"}]," — absent objects sampled uniformly from the COCO vocabulary (easiest)."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Popular"}]," — absent objects sampled from the most frequent COCO categories (stresses frequency bias)."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Adversarial"}]," — absent objects sampled from categories that co-occur with present objects (stresses co-occurrence bias — the failure mode this section is about)."]}],"\n"]}]
58:["$","p",null,{"children":"Scores are F1 over the yes/no answers; higher is better."}]

5f:["$","h4",null,{"children":"The Next 10,000 GPU-Hour Experiment"}]
60:["$","p",null,{"children":["If I were scoping a follow-up to LLaVA-1.5 with a 10,000 GPU-hour budget, I'd put it here: ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"replace the single CLIP encoder with a fused SigLIP + DINOv2 + depth-supervised third stream, keep the GPT-4 VIT data pipeline, and add a localization-supervised auxiliary loss."}]]}]
61:["$","p",null,{"children":"Concretely:"}]
62:["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Use Prismatic's fused-encoder recipe as the visual backbone (already shown to transfer to robotics via OpenVLA)."}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Augment the VIT corpus with grounded-language instructions that reference bounding boxes explicitly, forcing the projector + LLM to learn spatial tokens rather than global summaries."}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Add a lightweight dense-depth head co-trained from a monocular depth teacher (DPT, Marigold, or similar) so the token stream carries scale information CLIP discards."}],"\n"]}]
63:["$","p",null,{"children":"The goal is to close the exact gap that surfaces on POPE Adversarial (co-occurrence hallucination) and CHAIR (mentioned-but-absent objects) — both symptoms of underconstrained spatial grounding."}]

65:["$","hr",null,{}]
66:["$","h2",null,{"children":"Part II. Prismatic: Investigating the Design Space"}]
67:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Problem Domain & Taxonomy"}]
68:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Context"}]
69:["$","p",null,{"children":["By 2024, the VLM literature had converged on a broad template — vision encoder + projector + LLM, trained with a language-modeling loss on instruction data — but each paper (LLaVA, InstructBLIP, IDEFICS, MiniGPT-4, Fuyu-8B) held different axes fixed.\nNo controlled ablation existed over the axes themselves, so the field was publishing isolated design points without knowing which choices were load-bearing.\nPrismatic positions itself as that controlled audit.\nIts contribution is less a new architecture than a ",["$","em",null,{"children":"methodology"}],": a reproducible sweep over the VLM design space with every choice made explicit.\nThe headline result, the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"PRISM"}]," model family outperforming LLaVA-1.5 across 12 VLM benchmarks, is a byproduct.\nThe actual addition to the field is the ablation itself."]}]
6a:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Taxonomy of Approaches"}]
6b:["$","p",null,{"children":"Prismatic organizes the VLM design space along five axes and tests each in a controlled setting against a shared evaluation harness:"}]
6c:["$","table",null,{"children":[["$","thead",null,{"children":["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","th",null,{"children":"Axis"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"Question"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"Prismatic's finding (preview)"}]]}]}],["$","tbody",null,{"children":[["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Optimization"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Single vs. multi-stage; freeze or fine-tune encoder"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Single-stage wins (−20–25% compute); freeze wins on VLM benchmarks"}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Visual representation"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Contrastive (CLIP/SigLIP), self-supervised (DINOv2), or fusion?"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Fusion wins across the board."}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Language backbone"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Base LLM (Llama-2) vs. instruct-tuned (Vicuna)?"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Roughly tied; base hallucinates less."}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Image preprocessing"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Resize-and-crop, letterbox pad, or naive warp?"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Naive warp wins by a small margin."}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Scale"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"What resolution? What LLM size? How much data?"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"384px is the sweet spot; diminishing returns above."}]]}]]}]]}]
6d:["$","p",null,{"children":["Structurally, the claim this audit makes is partly ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"negative"}],": several popular choices (instruct-tuned LLMs, letterbox padding, multi-stage training) turn out not to matter or actively hurt, and at least one (fine-tuning the vision encoder) turns out to hurt on their benchmarks.\nThe positive claims (fusion wins, 384px wins, single-stage wins) are the headline; the negative claims reset defaults for the next generation of VLMs."]}]
6e:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Comparative Architectural Deep-Dive"}]
6f:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Objective Functions"}]
70:["$","p",null,{"children":["Prismatic keeps the LLaVA-style single objective: next-token cross-entropy over the concatenated ",["$","code",null,{"children":"[visual tokens || text tokens]"}]," sequence.\nNo contrastive loss, no region-level auxiliary losses, no Q-Former bottleneck.\nThis is a deliberate choice.\nIf the loss is held fixed across every ablation, any performance delta across the five axes is attributable to architecture, data, or optimization procedure rather than to the objective itself.\nIt is also the choice that limits the paper's scope (see §5 Load-Bearing Assumptions — every conclusion is conditioned on the patch-as-token autoregressive family from Part I's taxonomy)."]}]
71:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Backbone Scaling"}]
72:["$","h5",null,{"children":"Ensemble visual representations: SigLIP + DINOv2 fusion"}]
73:["$","p",null,{"children":"Prismatic's central architectural bet is that no single vision encoder captures everything a VLM needs.\nCLIP and SigLIP are trained with a vision-language contrastive objective and excel at semantic alignment but discard information orthogonal to text matching.\nDINOv2 is trained self-supervised, with no text, and retains dense pixel-level geometry but has no grounding to language.\nPrismatic fuses them by concatenation along the feature axis:"}]

75:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","img",null,{"src":"./figures/prismatic_fusion.png","alt":"Prismatic dual-encoder fusion: SigLIP (vision-language contrastive) provides semantic features Z_{\\text{sem}} with text grounding; DINOv2 (self-supervised) provides geometric features Z_{\\text{geo}} with pixel correspondence. Concatenation along the feature axis produces Z_{\\text{fused}}, preserving both semantic and spatial information for the LLM."}]}]
76:["$","p",null,{"children":["SigLIP (contrastive) provides text grounding but discards fine spatial detail; DINOv2 (self-supervised) preserves dense local features but lacks text alignment.\nThe fusion preserves ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"local pixel correspondences"}]," essential for:"]}]
77:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Object localization (RefCOCO)"}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"Spatial reasoning (VSR)"}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["Robotic manipulation (",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-openvla","children":"OpenVLA"}]," uses the Prismatic backbone)"]}],"\n"]}]
78:["$","p",null,{"children":["This directly addresses the information-decay bottleneck flagged in Part I — the LLaVA rank-bound theorem showed that a single CLIP encoder's feature dimensionality (1024) caps the information the projector can deliver to the LLM.\nPrismatic's fusion bypasses that cap by ",["$","em",null,{"children":"widening"}]," the input channel before projection rather than by adding projector rank."]}]
79:["$","h5",null,{"children":"RQ3: Which visual representation?"}]
7a:["$","p",null,{"children":"Prismatic ablates visual backbones systematically across VQA, localization, and challenge benchmarks (see Tables 2–4 in the paper's Appendix B.3 for exhaustive results).\nThe pattern is clear: DINOv2 alone excels at localization (RefCOCO, OCID-Ref) but lags on VQA; SigLIP alone does the reverse.\nFusing DINOv2 + SigLIP wins across the board, with the largest gains on localization tasks.\nThis confirms the semantic-vs-geometric split the fusion is designed to resolve — contrastive encoders (SigLIP) provide text grounding, self-supervised encoders (DINOv2) preserve spatial structure, and neither alone captures both."}]
7b:["$","h5",null,{"children":"RQ4: Base vs. instruct-tuned LLM?"}]
7c:["$","p",null,{"children":["Llama-2 (base) ≈ Vicuna v1.5 (instruct) on benchmarks, but ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"base has lower hallucination on POPE"}],".\nThis is a small, under-advertised finding with real downstream implications: Vicuna's instruction tuning was imported into LLaVA-1.5 as a default, but Prismatic shows it adds hallucination without adding accuracy.\nPrismatic's RQ4 suggests instruct-tuned LLMs provide no advantage, potentially simplifying future VLM recipes."]}]
7d:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Interaction Mechanisms"}]
7e:["$","h5",null,{"children":"Projector architecture"}]
7f:["$","p",null,{"children":"Prismatic inherits LLaVA-1.5's MLP projector and concatenation-into-LLM-context pattern — the \"patch-as-token\" family from Part I's taxonomy.\nThere is no cross-attention, no Q-Former, no register tokens, no architectural variant that would place the paper outside that family.\nThe paper explicitly flags this as a scope limitation (see §5)."}]
80:["$","h5",null,{"children":"RQ2: Freeze or fine-tune vision encoder?"}]
81:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Freeze wins."}],"\nFull fine-tuning significantly degrades performance, especially on spatial tasks (RefCOCO, OCID-Ref).\nPrismatic notes this contradicts closed-source work like Fuyu-8B that fine-tunes the backbone successfully, and the paper does not fully resolve the disagreement.\nIt offers two candidate explanations: (a) the scale and diversity of their vision-language training data may be insufficient to support fine-tuning without overfitting, and (b) a pure language-generation objective may discourage the fine-grained perceptual features a vision backbone needs to retain.\nBoth explanations remain live — §4 revisits this tension through OpenVLA Table 10, which ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"inverts"}]," the finding once the downstream target is action generation rather than text."]}]
82:["$","h5",null,{"children":"RQ5: Image preprocessing?"}]
83:["$","p",null,{"children":["Naive resize (warp to square, with aspect-ratio distortion) outperforms both letterbox padding (+0.5% over baseline) and resize-and-crop, winning by +1.2%.\nThe finding is small in magnitude but conceptually interesting: LLaVA-1.5 paid engineering cost to preserve aspect ratio via letterboxing on the grounds that distortion should hurt downstream reasoning.\nPrismatic shows the VLM is robust to distortion, and likely robust to it ",["$","em",null,{"children":"because"}]," the training data also contains it."]}]
84:["$","h3",null,{"children":"The Scaling Frontier & Empirical Trends"}]
85:["$","p",null,{"children":["Prismatic's contribution to the scaling frontier is not about parameter count — they hold model size roughly constant at 7B / 13B LLMs — but about ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"training-recipe compute efficiency"}],".\nTwo of the five design-space axes are scaling-flavored (optimization procedure, resolution), and both yield actionable compute-optimal findings."]}]
86:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Scaling Laws (training-recipe edition)"}]
87:["$","h5",null,{"children":"RQ1: Multi-stage vs. single-stage?"}]
88:["$","p",null,{"children":["LLaVA-1.5 splits training into two stages: stage 1 trains the projector only on 558K caption pairs (vision encoder + LLM frozen); stage 2 trains the projector and LLM together on 665K instruction-tuning samples.\nPrismatic compares this against a single-stage recipe (projector + LLM trained jointly from the start, vision encoder frozen throughout) and finds ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"no benefit from multi-stage"}],".\nSingle-stage saves ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"20–25% of training cost"}]," with no accuracy loss (Karamcheti et al., §4.1).\nThe multi-stage pipeline was a load-bearing assumption imported from BLIP-2 / InstructBLIP without empirical justification — Prismatic is the paper that broke it."]}]
89:["$","h5",null,{"children":"RQ6: Resolution scaling?"}]
8a:["$","p",null,{"children":["384px significantly outperforms 224px (+3–5% across benchmarks).\nDiminishing returns beyond 384px for standard tasks.\nThis mirrors LLaVA-1.5's 336px finding from Part I: both papers converge on the observation that ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"spatial resolution is under-exploited"}]," in the default 224px contrastive-pretrained backbone, and both pay a roughly quadratic token-count cost for the gain (336² vs 224² ≈ 2.25× tokens; 384² vs 224² ≈ 2.94×)."]}]
8b:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Diminishing Returns"}]
8c:["$","p",null,{"children":"The ablation tables show the familiar plateau pattern on every axis:"}]
8d:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Encoder fusion:"}]," +4–6% over any single encoder; no further encoders tested."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Resolution:"}]," +3–5% for 224→384px; minimal gains above 384px."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Single-stage compute savings:"}]," 20–25% saved with no accuracy cost — pure Pareto improvement."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Preprocessing:"}]," ~1% between the worst and best of three tested methods."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"LLM choice:"}]," base vs instruct-tuned is within noise on VQA-style metrics."]}],"\n"]}]
8e:["$","p",null,{"children":"Two of these matter (fusion, resolution); two are small (preprocessing, LLM choice); one is free money (single-stage).\nThe paper's lasting contribution is that it forces the field to stop treating the small ones as important and the free one as unreachable."}]
8f:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Robotic Grounding & Physicality Gap"}]
90:["$","h4",null,{"children":"The Precision Gap"}]
91:["$","p",null,{"children":["Prismatic itself is evaluated only on static VLM benchmarks, not on a robot.\nThe paper makes no claims about inference latency, action-space output, or closed-loop control.\nIts robotic relevance is established ",["$","em",null,{"children":"downstream"}],", through ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-openvla","children":"OpenVLA"}],"'s adoption of the Prismatic backbone as its vision-language front-end.\nThis matters for how the paper's claims should be read: RQ3 says \"fusion wins\" on static localization benchmarks, not on closed-loop manipulation; the downstream transfer is an empirical question, not a given."]}]
92:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Benchmark Critique"}]
93:["$","p",null,{"children":["The 12-benchmark suite behind the PRISM-beats-LLaVA headline is drawn from static VLM evals: VQAv2, GQA, VizWiz, TextVQA, POPE, MM-Vet, MMBench, SEED, RefCOCO, OCID-Ref, VSR, and AI2D.\nThese probe image understanding, grounding, and hallucination in a static, one-turn setting.\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"None of them require closed-loop motor action, temporal reasoning across frames, or physical interaction."}]," The same evaluation critique from Part I applies, inherited.\nThe downstream-side evaluator (OpenVLA's policy-success on BridgeData V2) is the closest the Prismatic backbone ever gets to a physicality-aware benchmark, and even that is a policy-level comparison rather than a pure VLM eval."]}]
94:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Engineering Bottlenecks"}]
95:["$","h5",null,{"children":"Inference reality (dual-encoder overhead)"}]
96:["$","p",null,{"children":"The SigLIP + DINOv2 fusion doubles the vision forward pass.\nEstimated combined cost is ~400–500 GFLOPs for the dual encoder alone (each ViT-L-class encoder at 384px runs ~200–250 GFLOPs).\nOn Jetson AGX Orin (~67 TFLOPS FP16 peak), the vision encoders alone could theoretically run at 100+ FPS, but real-world efficiency (memory bandwidth, utilization) typically drops this to 10–30 FPS.\nThe bottleneck is the 7B LLM: decoding even a short response adds 1–2 TFLOPs, pushing full-model throughput well below the ~10 Hz typical robotic-control budget.\nThis is the engineering cost of buying the localization gains documented in RQ3."}]
97:["$","h5",null,{"children":"Downstream robotic use (OpenVLA BridgeData V2)"}]
98:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-openvla","children":"OpenVLA"}]," (Kim et al., 2024) uses Prismatic as its vision-language backbone.\nTheir §3.1 VLM-backbone ablation compared Prismatic, LLaVA, and IDEFICS-1 — each fine-tuned as a policy — on five language-grounding tasks in the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"BridgeData V2 sink environment"}],", scored as ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"absolute success rate"}],":"]}]
99:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":"LLaVA beat IDEFICS-1 by ~35% on multi-object grounding."}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["Prismatic beat LLaVA by ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"~10%"}]," across both single- and multi-object tasks."]}],"\n"]}]
9a:["$","p",null,{"children":"OpenVLA attributes the Prismatic gain to the fused SigLIP + DINOv2 backbone's improved spatial reasoning (echoing Prismatic §4.2's ensembling result) and does not provide a failure-mode breakdown.\nCaveat: this is a policy-level comparison, not a pure VLM eval — Prismatic's advantage here bundles vision-grounding with OpenVLA's separate action tokenization, proprioceptive integration, and temporal modeling."}]
9b:["$","h5",null,{"children":"Fine-tune vs. freeze across scale and objective"}]
9c:["$","p",null,{"children":["OpenVLA also ablates fine-tuning vs. freezing the vision encoder, and the finding ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"inverts Prismatic's RQ2"}],".\nFine-tuning the encoder is crucial for policy performance (",["$","strong",null,{"children":"80.0% avg success"}]," vs. ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"46.7% frozen"}],"), with some frozen runs discontinued for near-zero success and unstable robot behavior.\nPrismatic §4.1 ablated this on VLM benchmarks and found the opposite, flagging the disagreement with prior work as unresolved.\nOpenVLA Table 10 is a concrete piece of that resolution from the downstream side — the recipe flips at the point where the model has to produce ",["$","em",null,{"children":"actions"}],", not just text.\nThis is the audit's cleanest case of a static-benchmark conclusion failing to transfer to embodied deployment, and it invalidates the simpler reading of Prismatic's methodology (run the sweep once, apply the recipe everywhere)."]}]
9d:["$","p",null,{"children":["Part III's ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-qwen3vl","children":"Qwen3-VL"}]," provides the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"scale-side"}]," resolution.\nIts pretraining runs four stages: S0 trains only the merger (vision encoder + LLM frozen, 67B tokens); S1 ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"unfreezes the full stack"}]," and trains on ~1T multimodal tokens; S2 and S3 continue with all parameters unfrozen through 32K and 256K context, adding ~1.1T more tokens.\nThe objective is language-generation throughout — the same objective Prismatic used and held fixed across its design-space sweep.\nThe only variable that changes is data scale: Prismatic trained at the LLaVA-1.5 data scale (~1.2M VL pairs); Qwen3-VL's full-fine-tune phase (S1–S3) exceeds 2T tokens.\nUnder a pure language-generation objective, at that scale, fine-tuning the vision encoder is not merely viable — it is the default.\nThis is the cleanest empirical support for Prismatic's own candidate explanation (a) from RQ2: insufficient scale and diversity of their vision-language training data as the reason freezing won in their setup.\nQwen3-VL doesn't resolve the question by contradiction; it resolves it by removing the data-starved regime that made freezing the right answer."]}]
9e:["$","p",null,{"children":["Taken together: Prismatic freezes (small VL data, text objective) and freezing wins; OpenVLA fine-tunes (action objective, ~970K ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-openx","children":"OpenX"}]," episodes) and fine-tuning wins; Qwen3-VL fine-tunes (text objective at trillion-token scale) and fine-tuning is the default.\nThe regime finding: ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"freezing is a conclusion of a data-starved, text-output regime"}],".\nSwap the objective to action generation (OpenVLA) or scale the data by ~10³× (Qwen3-VL), and the recipe flips.\nFor 2025/2026-era projects, Prismatic's RQ2 is guidance bounded by its training-data scale."]}]
9f:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Critical Synthesis & The \"Load-Bearing\" Audit"}]
a0:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Load-Bearing Assumptions"}]
a1:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Static VLM benchmarks capture what matters."}],"\nThe entire controlled sweep is adjudicated by VQA-style metrics on the 12-benchmark suite.\nIf instruction-tuned chatbots measure the wrong thing for downstream use, then \"which encoder wins?\" answers a question that doesn't transfer — exactly the failure mode OpenVLA Table 10 surfaces for RQ2."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Patch-as-token is the right frame."}],"\n§3.1 of the Prismatic paper explicitly admits no cross-attention variant was tested.\nIf the cross-attention family (Flamingo, BLIP-2) has different sensitivities to the ablated axes, every conclusion in the paper is conditioned on a single architectural family — and the methodology's generality claim collapses."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Freezing the encoder generalizes."}],"\nThis assumption is inverted downstream (see regime analysis above)."]}],"\n"]}]
a2:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Foundational vs. Marginal Gains"}]
a3:["$","p",null,{"children":["Prismatic's ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"foundational"}]," contribution is the methodology: a reproducible, controlled sweep over the VLM design space, with every choice made explicit.\nThe individual RQ findings split along a familiar axis — some surface real asymmetries (RQ1 compute savings, RQ3 fusion wins, RQ6 resolution sweet spot), others surface choices that don't matter (RQ4 base-vs-instruct, RQ5 preprocessing).\nThe most consequential result in retrospect is ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"not"}]," an architectural ablation; it's the procedural one — ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"single-stage training works"}]," — which changed the default recipe for every VLM that cited Prismatic and turned out to be free compute for the entire field."]}]
a4:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Failure Modes"}]
a5:["$","h5",null,{"children":"Acknowledged limitations (§3.1 of the Prismatic paper)"}]
a6:["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Architecture scope:"}]," Only explores \"patch-as-token\" fully autoregressive architecture (no cross-attention variants)."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Evaluation scope:"}]," Limited to established benchmarks; no human evaluation or real-world interaction studies."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"TextVQA regression:"}]," DINOv2 fusion hurts Optical Character Recognition (OCR)-heavy tasks (mitigated but not eliminated by SigLIP)."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Compute overhead:"}]," 2× vision encoder cost may be prohibitive for resource-constrained deployment."]}],"\n"]}]
a7:["$","h5",null,{"children":"Inherited failure modes"}]
a8:["$","p",null,{"children":"From the vision encoders:"}]
a9:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"SigLIP:"}]," Same contrastive-training limitations as CLIP — discards information orthogonal to text matching."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"DINOv2:"}]," No text grounding; may retain irrelevant low-level detail."]}],"\n"]}]
aa:["$","p",null,{"children":"From the LLM:"}]
ab:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Hallucination:"}]," Still present (though reduced vs. instruct-tuned LLMs, per RQ4)."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Single-frame:"}]," No temporal reasoning (addressed downstream by OpenVLA and Qwen3-VL)."]}],"\n"]}]
ac:["$","h4",null,{"children":"The Next 10,000 GPU-Hour Experiment"}]
ad:["$","p",null,{"children":["If I were scoping a follow-up to Prismatic with a 10,000 GPU-hour budget, I'd put it here: ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"run a parallel design-space sweep that extends the architectural-family axis and replaces the static-benchmark adjudicator with a downstream robotic evaluator."}]]}]
ae:["$","p",null,{"children":"This directly tests the two load-bearing assumptions Prismatic cannot interrogate: (1) does \"patch-as-token\" generalize across architectural families, and (2) do VLM-benchmark winners survive the static to closed-loop transition?\nThe RQ2 inversion in OpenVLA Table 10 is the clearest evidence: freezing wins on static benchmarks, fine-tuning wins on policy success.\nA follow-up sweep should make that inversion systematic rather than incidental."}]
af:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","strong",null,{"children":"Concretely:"}]}]
b0:["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Extend the architecture axis (~3,000 GPU-hours)."}],"\nAdd at least one cross-attention variant (BLIP-2-style Q-Former) and one register-token variant (DINOv2 + registers) to the existing patch-as-token family.\nTrain each under Prismatic's single-stage recipe with the same data mixture.\nThis fills the gap §3.1 explicitly flags: \"no cross-attention variant was tested.\""]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Replace the adjudicator (~2,000 GPU-hours)."}],"\nSwap the 12-benchmark static VLM suite for a downstream robotic evaluator: OpenVLA-style policy fine-tuning on BridgeData V2 (5 language-grounding tasks) plus a held-out sim environment (SIMPLER or LIBERO) to test generalization.\nScore by absolute success rate, not VQA accuracy."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Ablate freeze-vs-fine-tune across both axes (~4,000 GPU-hours)."}],"\nFor each architecture variant × {freeze, fine-tune} × {static benchmarks, policy success}, record the full matrix.\nThis isolates whether the RQ2 inversion is specific to action-generation objectives (OpenVLA's explanation) or generalizes to any closed-loop evaluator."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Measure inference latency (~1,000 GPU-hours for profiling)."}],"\nFor each variant, report tokens/sec on Orin-class hardware and estimate maximum control frequency.\nThe dual-encoder overhead (§Engineering Bottlenecks: ~350–400 GFLOPs, <1 Hz on Orin) is a first-order constraint for robotics; the sweep should quantify whether cross-attention or register-token variants change this tradeoff."]}],"\n"]}],"\n"]}]
b1:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","strong",null,{"children":"Success metrics:"}]}]
b2:["$","table",null,{"children":[["$","thead",null,{"children":["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","th",null,{"children":"Metric"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"Target"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"Rationale"}]]}]}],["$","tbody",null,{"children":[["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"BridgeData V2 avg success"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"≥ OpenVLA baseline (~80% with Prismatic backbone)"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Confirms architectural variant doesn't regress policy transfer"}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"SIMPLER/LIBERO generalization"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"≥ 60% success on held-out tasks"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Tests sim-to-sim transfer, not just BridgeData overfitting"}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Freeze-vs-fine-tune delta"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Quantified ± for each (architecture, evaluator) cell"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"The main deliverable: a regime map, not a single recommendation"}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":"Inference on Orin"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"≥ 5 Hz for at least one variant"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Minimum viable for low-frequency manipulation; flags which variants are edge-deployable"}]]}]]}]]}]
b3:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"What this resolves:"}]," If cross-attention variants show a different freeze/fine-tune sensitivity than patch-as-token, Prismatic's RQ2 finding is family-specific, not universal.\nIf the inversion replicates across all families but only under policy evaluation, the field learns that static VLM benchmarks are fundamentally misaligned with robotic deployment — a stronger negative result than OpenVLA's single data point."]}]
b4:["$","h2",null,{"children":"Part III. Qwen3-VL: Native Multimodality & Reasoning"}]
b5:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Novelty & Contribution"}]
b6:["$","p",null,{"children":["As of early 2026, ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Qwen3-VL"}]," exemplifies the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"native multimodality"}]," paradigm at scale, treating images and videos as interleaved tokens in a massive ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"256K-token window"}],"."]}]
b7:["$","p",null,{"children":"Its \"Thinking Mode\" builds on DeepSeek-R1's chain-of-thought paradigm but differs architecturally:"}]
b8:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Unified weights, switchable modes."}],"\nUnlike Qwen2.5 (which required swapping to a dedicated reasoning model like QwQ-32B), Qwen3 integrates ",["$","em",null,{"children":"non-thinking"}]," and ",["$","em",null,{"children":"thinking"}]," modes in one checkpoint.\nIn thinking mode, the model generates inside ",["$","code",null,{"children":"<thinking>"}]," tags; a ",["$","em",null,{"children":"thinking budget"}]," allocates compute adaptively based on query complexity."]}]
b9:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Multimodal extension."}],"\nQwen3-VL extends thinking mode to vision-language: multimodal reasoning, spatial/video understanding, and visual coding.\nThis is the focus of this audit."]}]
ba:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Agentic deployment."}],"\nQwen3-VL optimizes thinking mode for tool-augmented tasks (GUI interaction, multi-tool routing, coding).\nBenchmarks: strong on SWE-Bench (coding), Tau2-Bench (agentic control), and advanced math reasoning.\nThe architecture scales from 4B dense to 235B-A22B MoE without structural changes."]}]
bb:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Architectural Summary: DeepStack & MRoPE"}]
bc:["$","p",null,{"children":"Qwen3-VL replaces the MLP bridge with a unified \"Omni-architecture\":"}]
bd:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":[["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-deepstack","children":"DeepStack"}]," Integration"]}],": Injects visual tokens from multiple intermediate layers of the ViT into the LLM to preserve multi-level abstractions."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Interleaved-MRoPE"}],": An enhanced Rotary Positional Embedding that allows for full-frequency allocation over width, height, and time, enabling unified spatial-temporal modeling."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Thinking Mode"}],": A unified reasoning capability (inspired by DeepSeek-R1) that runs CoT traces internally, optionally invoking tools (e.g., ",["$","code",null,{"children":"image_zoom_in_tool"}],") to verify visual details before outputting a final answer.\nThe same weights serve both reasoning and fast-response modes.\nTraining is three-stage: (1) SFT with CoT-format data activates reasoning, (2) strong-to-weak distillation transfers capabilities from a teacher model, (3) RL refines alignment.\nFor \"thinking with images,\" the pipeline adds cold-start SFT on ~10K grounding examples followed by multi-turn tool-integrated RL with three reward signals (answer accuracy, reasoning coherence, appropriate tool usage)."]}],"\n"]}]
be:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Chosen sub-domain:"}]," ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Ultra-long context multimodal transformers for video + document reasoning"}],"\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Primary paper:"}]," Qwen3-VL technical report\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Thesis of the audit:"}]," Qwen3-VL is a strong ",["$","em",null,{"children":"long-context multimodal reasoner"}]," enabled by (i) frequency-balanced positional encoding, (ii) multi-level cross-layer visual injection, and (iii) textual time grounding—",["$","strong",null,{"children":"but"}]," it leaves several load-bearing engineering questions unanswered (latency/FLOPs, token budgets vs resolution, fusion alignment details), and it does ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"not"}]," close the semantic→motor gap required for robotics-grade physicality."]}]
bf:["$","hr",null,{}]
c0:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Problem Domain & Why It Matters"}]
c1:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Problem:"}]," Build a ",["$","em",null,{"children":"single"}]," autoregressive multimodal model that can reason over ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"interleaved text+images+video"}]," at ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"256K tokens"}]," natively, and extrapolate to ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"~1M tokens"}]," (~2h video) without catastrophic decay in temporal localization, document retrieval, or language performance.\nQwen3-VL explicitly frames \"maintaining strong pure-text capabilities\" as part of the goal."]}]
c2:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","strong",null,{"children":"Why it’s hard (first principles):"}]}]
c3:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Long context"}]," amplifies compute cost and attention memory quadratically.\nStandard self-attention scales as ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":[["$","mi",null,{"children":"O"}],["$","mo",null,{"stretchy":"false","children":"("}],["$","msup",null,{"children":[["$","mi",null,{"children":"n"}],["$","mn",null,{"children":"2"}]]}],["$","mo",null,{"stretchy":"false","children":")"}]]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"O(n^2)"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"1.0641em","verticalAlign":"-0.25em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","style":{"marginRight":"0.0278em"},"children":"O"}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mopen","children":"("}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","children":"n"}],["$","span",null,{"className":"msupsub","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"vlist-t","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"vlist-r","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"vlist","style":{"height":"0.8141em"},"children":["$","span",null,{"style":{"top":"-3.063em","marginRight":"0.05em"},"children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"pstrut","style":{"height":"2.7em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mtight","children":"2"}]}]]}]}]}]}]}]]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mclose","children":")"}]]}]}]]}],": at 8K tokens the attention matrix is 67M entries; at 256K it's 69B entries (1024× larger).\nAt FP16, a 256K×256K attention matrix alone consumes ~128GB — exceeding single-GPU memory and requiring sharding, KV-cache compression, or sparse attention variants."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Video"}]," adds a time axis → positional encoding becomes a failure point."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Vision tokens"}]," can drown the LLM, both compute-wise and representationally (information bottleneck)."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Temporal grounding"}]," tends to become either (a) positional-ID hacks that don’t extrapolate, or (b) symbolic tokens that don’t preserve continuous dynamics."]}],"\n"]}]

c7:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Positional encoding for space–time (t,h,w)"}]
c8:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Camp A: Factorized rotary encoding (MRoPE-style)."}],"\nClassic MRoPE partitions embedding dimensions into temporal/spatial subspaces.\nQwen3-VL claims this creates an ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"imbalanced frequency spectrum"}]," that degrades long-video understanding."]}]
c9:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Camp B: Frequency-balanced / interleaved rotary."}],"\nQwen3-VL’s ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Interleaved MRoPE"}]," interleaves the t/h/w components across embedding dimensions so each axis spans both low and high frequencies."]}]
ca:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","img",null,{"src":"./figures/mrope_comparison.png","alt":"Factorized vs Interleaved MRoPE: factorized (Qwen2-VL) confines each axis (t,h,w) to a subset of embedding dimensions, creating an imbalanced frequency spectrum. Interleaved (Qwen3-VL) distributes all axes across the full dimension range, giving each axis access to both low and high frequencies for better long-video extrapolation."}]}]

d1:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Long-context training"}]

d9:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Model variants and parameter activation"}]

db:["$","blockquote",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Audit implication:"}]," The model family covers edge-to-cloud, but the report provides no latency benchmarks or FLOPs-per-token figures across variants.\nFor robotics deployment, the missing data point is: at what model size does inference hit 10–50 Hz on Orin-class hardware?"]}],"\n"]}]
dc:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Vision encoder choice and dynamic resolution"}]
dd:["$","p",null,{"children":["Qwen3-VL uses the ",["$","strong",null,{"children":["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-siglip2","children":"SigLIP-2"}]}]," architecture as the vision encoder, initialized from the official pretrained checkpoints, and continues training with ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"dynamic input resolutions"}]," using ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"2D-RoPE"}]," and interpolated absolute position embeddings (following ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-comp","children":"CoMP"}],").\nThe report defaults to ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"SigLIP2-SO-400M"}]," and substitutes ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"SigLIP2-Large (300M)"}]," for small-scale LLMs (2B and 4B).\nThe split follows an implicit capacity-matching rule — smaller LLM, smaller encoder — but the paper gives no explicit rationale beyond the assignment, and no ablation isolating the SO-400M / Large-300M choice."]}]
de:["$","p",null,{"children":["The paper ",["$","em",null,{"children":"does"}]," ablate the vision backbone on a different axis (Table 11): the authors' ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Qwen3-ViT"}]," — SigLIP-2 enhanced by continued dynamic-resolution training — vs. the original SigLIP-2.\nAt the CLIP pretraining stage, Qwen3-ViT is roughly parity on ImageNet-1K/V2/A/R (within ±0.4), regresses on ImageNet-S (76.2 → 74.5), and gains substantially on OmniBench (36.9 → 45.5), the authors' in-house world-knowledge eval.\nWhen paired with a 1.7B Qwen3 LLM and trained for 1.5T tokens, Qwen3-ViT pulls ahead on the downstream VLM suite as well: RealWorldQA 58.7 → 66.1, AI2D 74.1 → 76.2, OCRBench 77.2 → 78.7, InfoVQA 65.3 → 67.0, Omni 50.1 → ~53.\nThe ablation establishes that the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"additional"}]," dynamic-resolution training on top of SigLIP-2 helps; it does not establish which variant size is the right starting point."]}]

f9:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Relation to other skip/injection schemes"}]
fa:["$","p",null,{"children":["The \"DeepStack-style\" framing invites comparison with classical skip-connection patterns.\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"ResNet"}]," uses addition-based identity shortcuts within a single network for gradient flow.\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"U-Net"}]," uses concatenation-based shortcuts at matched resolutions for spatial detail preservation.\nDeepStack borrows ResNet's addition operation but applies it ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"cross-network"}]," (vision encoder → LLM), and borrows U-Net's multi-resolution intuition but indexes by encoder ",["$","em",null,{"children":"depth"}]," (low/mid/high-level features) rather than spatial resolution."]}]
fb:["$","p",null,{"children":"Three cross-network injection patterns dominate VLMs:"}]
fc:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"LLaVA / prefix"}]," — visual tokens concatenated at layer 0 only; information propagates via self-attention."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Flamingo / cross-attention"}]," — learned cross-attention layers interleaved every ",["$","span",null,{"className":"katex","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-mathml","children":["$","math",null,{"xmlns":"http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML","children":["$","semantics",null,{"children":[["$","mrow",null,{"children":["$","mi",null,{"children":"k"}]}],["$","annotation",null,{"encoding":"application/x-tex","children":"k"}]]}]}]}],["$","span",null,{"className":"katex-html","aria-hidden":"true","children":["$","span",null,{"className":"base","children":[["$","span",null,{"className":"strut","style":{"height":"0.6944em"}}],["$","span",null,{"className":"mord mathnormal","style":{"marginRight":"0.0315em"},"children":"k"}]]}]}]]}]," LLM blocks; expressive but expensive."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"DeepStack"}]," — projected vision features added directly to hidden states at multiple shallow LLM layers; cheaper than cross-attention, richer than single-point prefix."]}],"\n"]}]
fd:["$","p",null,{"children":"DeepStack is the middle ground: multi-level vision features at multiple LLM depths, injected via addition rather than attention."}]
fe:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Innovation or marketing?"}],"\nDeepStack-the-concept is from ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-deepstack","children":"DeepStack (Meng et al., 2024)"}],", not this paper.\nQwen3-VL's contribution is the specific instantiation (three vision levels → first three LLM layers, projection-then-add), not a new primitive."]}]

118:["$","blockquote",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Audit critique:"}]," This likely improves time-localization tasks (grounding, dense captioning), but it makes time ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"linguistic"}]," rather than a continuous latent tied to dynamics.\nGreat for QA; potentially weak for control."]}],"\n"]}]
119:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Alternatives the field has explored"}]
11a:["$","p",null,{"children":"The linguistic-time choice isn't forced — two concrete alternatives sit closer to a continuous-time representation, each addressing a different layer of the stack:"}]
11b:["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{}],"\n"]}]
11c:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":["Continuous-time positional encoding (",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-qwen25vl","children":"Qwen2.5-VL"}],"'s time-synced MRoPE)."]}]," The immediate predecessor encoded actual video time via rotary frequencies.\nQwen3-VL's own justification for moving away is sparsity at long horizons (\"sparse, huge temporal IDs\" and costly fps-uniform sampling — §4.2 opening).\nThe tradeoff is explicit in the report: linguistic time trades temporal-resolution fidelity for compute scalability.\nThat is a scalability argument, not a control-fidelity argument."]}]
11d:["$","ol",null,{"start":"2","children":["\n",["$","li",null,{}],"\n"]}]
11e:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":["Continuous-time action heads (",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-octo","children":"Octo"}]," / ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-rt2","children":"RT-2"}]," / ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-openvla","children":"OpenVLA"}],")."]}]," The VLA family inherits the VLM backbone's tokenization on the input side but handles time continuously on the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"output"}]," side: action chunks, learned step embeddings, horizon-conditioned rollout.\nThis pattern concedes Qwen3-VL's input-side discretization and pays for continuous time at the point that actually matters for motor control."]}]
11f:["$","p",null,{"children":"Neither fully resolves the semantic-motor gap on its own.\nThe practical pattern post-2024 has been to pair a linguistic-time VLM backbone with a continuous-time action head (option 2) — which makes Qwen3-VL's input-side choice compatible with eventual robotic deployment, but only if the action-head adaptation happens downstream.\nThis is the same structural argument Part II §4 makes via OpenVLA: the VLM isn't the policy, and bolting a continuous-action interface on is the step the report defers."}]

123:["$","p",null,{"children":["The largest gains cluster on document/OCR-heavy benchmarks (OCRBench +2.6, InfoVQA +2.3) — consistent with the architectural story that multi-level features preserve fine-grained spatial detail that a single-point prefix loses.\nWhat this establishes: ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"cross-layer injection helps, with the gain concentrated where multi-scale spatial features matter."}]," What it does ",["$","em",null,{"children":"not"}]," isolate: whether the gain comes from (a) multi-level vision features, (b) multi-depth LLM injection, or (c) the combination.\nA finer-grained ablation — varying just the vision-encoder levels tapped, or just the LLM layers injected into, while holding the other fixed — would pin down which of the three design choices is actually load-bearing.\nThe ablation also runs on a surrogate 15B-A2B model, not the flagship MoE, so the +1.3 AVG gain transfers to the shipped system only under the assumption that the architectural signal is scale-invariant — a reasonable guess but not a tested one.\nThe reported ablation is consistent with DeepStack-as-architecture-improvement but does not validate the specific hyperparameter choice (three levels / first three layers) as compute-optimal."]}]

126:["$","p",null,{"children":"Qwen3-VL's four-stage pretraining curriculum is easy to read as an implementation detail, but the numbers encode substantial empirical claims about long-context VLM training at frontier scale — the sort of claims that only emerge after running the full sweep and that the audit should foreground rather than transcribe:"}]
127:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"S0 (Alignment):"}]," train only the merger, freeze vision encoder + LLM; ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"67B tokens"}]," at ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"8,192"}],"."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"S1 (Full multimodal):"}]," unfreeze all; ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"~1T tokens"}]," at ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"8,192"}],"."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"S2 (Long context):"}]," all params; ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"~1T tokens"}]," at ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"32,768"}],"."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"S3 (Ultra-long):"}]," all params; ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"100B tokens"}]," at ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"262,144 (256K)"}],"."]}],"\n"]}]
128:["$","p",null,{"children":["Three load-bearing findings sit inside these numbers.\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"First, the token allocation is ~3% / 46% / 46% / 5%"}]," (67B + 1T + 1T + 100B ≈ 2.17T total): the substantive training happens at 8K and 32K context; 256K is a curriculum capstone, not a volume-dominant stage.\nThe empirical claim is that you do not need 256K-native training for most of the run to get 256K-native behavior — a load-bearing result for any 2025/2026 project trying to build long-context VLMs at lower compute than Qwen's own.\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Second, S0 alone is ~67B tokens"}],", dwarfing the VL-pair-scale corpus Prismatic trained on (Part II §4 documents the scale gap).\nThe alignment stage is no longer a \"warmup\" — at frontier scale it is where the merger learns to align independently-trained encoders, and its size is itself a finding.\n",["$","strong",null,{"children":"Third, the S3 interleaving recipe"}]," — one epoch at 32K then one at 256K with 32K samples interleaved (including \"videos up to two hours\") — preserves short-context performance while extending to 256K native, a problem the pre-2024 literature typically handled post-hoc via ",["$","a",null,{"href":"#ref-yarn","children":"YaRN"}],"-style extrapolation rather than native training (Qwen3-VL uses YaRN only to extrapolate past its 256K native window to ~1M)."]}]
129:["$","p",null,{"children":"Note that S1–S3 all train with the full stack unfrozen — the opposite of Prismatic's RQ2 \"freeze wins\" recipe.\nPart II §4 frames this as the scale-side resolution of the freeze-vs-fine-tune debate; see there for the three-point comparison against OpenVLA's action-objective flip."}]
12a:["$","blockquote",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Load-bearing missing detail:"}]," the report presents this curriculum as a recipe but does not publish the ablations that would justify the specific allocations (67B / 1T / 1T / 100B, or the 32K-interleave ratio).\nThe ",["$","em",null,{"children":"numbers"}]," are the finding; without sensitivity analysis, we cannot distinguish \"this is compute-optimal\" from \"this is what Qwen3-VL shipped.\"\nFor a 2025/2026-era project that wants to replicate the long-context behavior at lower scale, the curriculum is a starting point, not a proven optimum."]}],"\n"]}]

132:["$","blockquote",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Audit conclusion:"}]," you cannot sign off on this for edge real-time control (e.g., Orin-class) based on this report alone."]}],"\n"]}]
133:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Dimensionality & Information Decay: the merger bottleneck"}]
134:["$","p",null,{"children":["The merger compresses 2×2 SigLIP-2 patch features into ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"one visual token"}]," via MLP — a 4× per-image token-count reduction that compounds per video frame.\nThe audit's concern here is narrow and specific: ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"the report never ablates this compression ratio."}]," DeepStack-style cross-layer injection (§3.3) partially compensates by delivering multi-level features to the first three LLM layers, but the merger still sets the hard upper bound on spatial resolution reaching the LLM.\nFor tasks where affordance-level geometry lives below the merger's effective resolution, this is where the model loses information that a policy downstream would need."]}]
135:["$","p",null,{"children":["The testable prediction this sharpens to: ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"at fixed LLM and fixed input resolution, downstream spatial-grounding accuracy (RefCOCO / OCID-Ref) is a monotonic function of the merger compression ratio, and the 2×2 default is chosen without empirical justification."}]," Prismatic established that merger-style design axes routinely get assumed rather than ablated across the VLM literature (Part II §2); this is a direct instance, and the missing ablation is what would turn \"bottleneck concern\" into a quantified risk."]}]
136:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Semantic-motor gap: reasoning ≠ motor primitives"}]
137:["$","p",null,{"children":["The report frames Qwen3-VL as an \"engine for embodied AI agents\" (§6 opening), but the architecture it describes emits a stream of text tokens.\nThere is no action head, no action-tokenization scheme, and no embodied policy training stage in the four-stage pretraining curriculum (§5).\nThe GUI-agent data in the training mix demonstrates that Qwen3-VL can operate over ",["$","em",null,{"children":"discrete, symbolic"}]," action spaces (click targets, key events) — that is not the same interface as the ",["$","em",null,{"children":"continuous motor commands"}]," a physical robot needs, and the report does not bridge the two."]}]
138:["$","p",null,{"children":["Part II §4 is where this audit already established what closing that gap costs: OpenVLA's fine-tune-vs-freeze inversion (80.0% vs. 46.7%) showed that turning even a strong VLM backbone into a policy requires separate action tokenization and policy-level fine-tuning, and the training recipe flips at that transition.\nQwen3-VL inherits none of that adaptation work — it ships as a text-output model.\nAny \"embodied agent\" framing of Qwen3-VL therefore depends on a ",["$","em",null,{"children":"downstream"}]," OpenVLA-style adaptation that the report does not specify or evaluate."]}]
139:["$","blockquote",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Audit takeaway:"}]," The report's embodiment framing runs ahead of its architecture.\nQwen3-VL is a state-of-the-art multimodal reasoner with text output; becoming a robot policy is deferred work, and Part II §4 quantifies the cost."]}],"\n"]}]
13a:["$","hr",null,{}]
13b:["$","h3",null,{"children":"Critical Synthesis: Load-Bearing Assumptions"}]
13c:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Long-context retrieval implies long-horizon physical understanding"}]
13d:["$","p",null,{"children":["Needle-in-a-haystack (99.5% at 1M tokens) shows long-range retrieval.\nBut retrieving ",["$","em",null,{"children":"where"}]," a frame occurred is not the same as predicting ",["$","em",null,{"children":"what happens next"}]," given contact, occlusion, or perturbation.\nThe architecture has no physics prior, no dynamics head, and no contact model — it predicts text tokens, not trajectories."]}]
13e:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Timestamp tokens create “precise temporal grounding”"}]
13f:["$","p",null,{"children":"The report claims “perceive temporal information more effectively and precisely” for grounding/dense captioning.\nBut textual time can become symbolic manipulation unless grounded in dynamics.\nSimilarly, cross-layer injection (DeepStack) is plausible and supported by ablations, but the exact alignment mechanism is under-specified."}]
140:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Foundational vs. Marginal Gains"}]
141:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","strong",null,{"children":"Foundational contributions:"}]}]
142:["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Interleaved MRoPE"}]," — The frequency-balanced positional encoding that distributes t/h/w axes across the full embedding dimension range (§Interleaved MRoPE above).\nThis directly addresses the spectral imbalance that degraded long-video understanding in Qwen2-VL's factorized scheme.\nThe architectural change is simple but the downstream effect — native 256K context with extrapolation to ~1M — is load-bearing for the entire long-context multimodal paradigm."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Textual timestamp grounding"}]," — Replacing time-synced positional IDs with linguistic tokens (",["$","code",null,{"children":"<3.0 seconds>"}],", HMS formats) is a design pivot, not an iteration.\nIt trades continuous-time fidelity for scalability at long horizons, enabling the 2-hour video regime without the sparse/huge temporal ID problem (§Video timestamp tokens).\nWhether this tradeoff is ",["$","em",null,{"children":"correct"}]," for robotics is contested (see §Robotic Grounding), but it is foundational to Qwen3-VL's scaling story."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Four-stage curriculum with 256K capstone"}]," — The finding that ~95% of training can happen at 8K–32K context while still achieving 256K-native behavior (§Training Details) is an empirical contribution the field inherits.\nThis is a recipe, not an architecture, but it's foundational for any 2025/2026 project attempting long-context VLMs at lower compute."]}],"\n"]}],"\n"]}]
143:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","strong",null,{"children":"Marginal gains (engineering refinements):"}]}]
144:["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"DeepStack cross-layer injection"}]," — The +1.3 AVG gain (Table 12) is real but concentrated on OCR/document tasks.\nThe architectural idea is borrowed from Meng et al. (2024), and the specific hyperparameter choice (three levels → first three LLM layers) is unablated.\nThis is a well-motivated instantiation, not an innovation."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"SigLIP-2 over SigLIP"}]," — Qwen3-ViT's continued dynamic-resolution training yields gains on OmniBench and downstream VLM benchmarks (Table 11), but the delta over vanilla SigLIP-2 is incremental encoder tuning, not a new representation paradigm."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Thinking mode"}]," — Unified weights for thinking/non-thinking is an engineering convenience (no model swap needed), but the underlying CoT-with-budget idea is inherited from DeepSeek-R1.\nThe multimodal extension is valuable but derivative."]}],"\n"]}],"\n"]}]
145:["$","h4",null,{"children":"Failure Modes"}]
146:["$","p",null,{"children":"The following failure modes are predictions derived from Qwen3-VL's documented architectural limitations:"}]
147:["$","table",null,{"children":[["$","thead",null,{"children":["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","th",null,{"children":"Failure Mode"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"Root Cause"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"Architectural Evidence"}],["$","th",null,{"children":"Potential Fix"}]]}]}],["$","tbody",null,{"children":[["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Sub-patch affordances"}]," (e.g., button edges, insertion slots, contact points smaller than ~28×28 px at native resolution)"]}],["$","td",null,{"children":"2×2 merger compression discards spatial detail below its effective resolution"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"§Merger bottleneck: “the report never ablates this compression ratio”; 4× token reduction per frame"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Higher-resolution visual tokens; skip the merger for policy-critical frames; auxiliary dense-prediction head"}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Continuous-time dynamics"}]," (e.g., catching a thrown object, predicting collision timing, velocity-dependent grasps)"]}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Linguistic timestamps discretize time into symbolic tokens, losing sub-second continuous dynamics"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"§Video timestamp tokens: “time becomes linguistic rather than a continuous latent tied to dynamics”"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Continuous-time action head (Octo/OpenVLA pattern); hybrid encoding with both symbolic and rotary time"}]]}],["$","tr",null,{"children":[["$","td",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Closed-loop motor control"}]," (any task requiring >1 Hz action feedback)"]}],["$","td",null,{"children":"No action tokenization, no policy head; model emits text tokens only"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"§Semantic-motor gap: “no action head, no action-tokenization scheme”; 22B activated params/token with no latency benchmarks"}],["$","td",null,{"children":"Downstream adapter (proposed in §Next 10K GPU-hours); distillation to smaller action-specialized decoder"}]]}]]}]]}]
148:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","strong",null,{"children":"Concrete scenario predictions:"}]}]
149:["$","ol",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Transparent/reflective object manipulation:"}]," Contrastive-trained encoders (CLIP, SigLIP, SigLIP-2) learn representations optimized for text-matching, which correlates strongly with texture and color patterns — cues that transparent and reflective surfaces lack.\nCombined with the 2×2 merger compression, Qwen3-VL will likely struggle to localize glass edges or mirrored surfaces where affordance geometry depends on specular cues.\nThis is an inference from the training objective, not a documented benchmark result; SigLIP-2's paper does not evaluate transparent-object performance."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"High-velocity interception:"}]," A task like “catch the ball” requires sub-100ms action latency and continuous trajectory prediction.\nQwen3-VL's linguistic timestamps (",["$","code",null,{"children":"<0.5 seconds>"}],") cannot represent the 50ms precision needed, and the 22B-activated MoE has no published latency that would confirm real-time feasibility.\nPrediction: the model can ",["$","em",null,{"children":"describe"}]," the catch but cannot ",["$","em",null,{"children":"execute"}]," it."]}],"\n"]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":["\n",["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Long-horizon causal chains with physical state change:"}]," The 256K context enables retrieval (“what happened at minute 47?”) but not simulation.\nA task like “the cup was knocked over earlier; where is the spill now?” requires physical state propagation the architecture has no mechanism for — no dynamics model, no world-state tracking beyond token attention.\nPrediction: correct retrieval of the knock-over event, incorrect inference about current spill location."]}],"\n"]}],"\n"]}]

159:["$","hr",null,{}]
15a:["$","h2",null,{"children":"Cross-Cutting Gap: What Happens When Internet-Scale Data Runs Out?"}]
15b:["$","p",null,{"children":"The LLaVA → Prismatic → Qwen3-VL trajectory implicitly assumes continued access to internet-scale multimodal data.\nThis assumption has a shelf life."}]
15c:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"The bottleneck isn't raw images — it's paired data."}],"\nStatic images are abundant (billions on the web), but ",["$","em",null,{"children":"high-quality instruction-following pairs"}]," are scarce.\nLLaVA's GPT-4-bootstrapped VIT corpus (158K samples) and Prismatic's SFT mixture (~1.2M pairs) are orders of magnitude smaller than the image pool they draw from.\nVideo is worse: captioned video at the quality level Qwen3-VL trains on (dense timestamps, multi-turn grounding) is far rarer than raw footage."]}]
15d:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Scaling laws bend when data saturates."}],"\nQwen3-VL's 2T+ token pretraining budget already approaches the frontier of public multimodal corpora.\nThe Chinchilla-optimal regime (compute ≈ data) assumes data is elastic; if it isn't, additional compute buys diminishing returns or overfitting.\nThe field has no published study on VLM behavior past this saturation point."]}]
15e:["$","p",null,{"children":["$","strong",null,{"children":"Potential mitigations (unvalidated at frontier scale):"}]}]
15f:["$","ul",null,{"children":["\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Synthetic data augmentation"}]," — generate pairs from unlabeled images/video (LLaVA's original move, scaled up); risks distribution collapse."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Active data mining"}]," — prioritize rare/hard examples; risks selection bias and diminishing returns."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Embodied data generation"}]," — robots generate trajectories (OpenX-style); expensive, domain-specific."]}],"\n",["$","li",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Cross-modal transfer"}]," — leverage language-only data; limited evidence for strong transfer at scale."]}],"\n"]}]
160:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Audit conclusion:"}]," The 2023–2025 VLM trajectory rode a data abundance wave that cannot continue indefinitely.\nNo paper in this audit addresses what happens when the curve flattens — the question is deferred, not answered."]}]
